Symbols + check ++ double check mate checkmate ! good move " excellent move ? bad move 7 blunder 1? interesting or risky move Nn dubious move 1-0 Black resigns 0-1 White resigns 2-12 draw agreed Ch. championship Preface ‘The Najdorf. The very name is cnough to fill 1 e4 players with fear, for this is one of the most uncompro- mising, yet sound, variations in the whole spectrum of chess openings: I feel that this book fully conforms to the title of the ‘Winning With . . . " se- rics. Most of the top players in the world have at one time or another played the Najdorf. Currently, the strongest exponents of the variation are Vassily Ivanchuk, Boris Gelfand and the World Champion himself, Gary Kasparov — though sadly, from the point of view of the Najdorf devotee, the opening has such a fear- some reputation in his hands that few of his opponents are brave enough to play 1 e4 against him. My approach in this book has been selective rather than compre- hensive. Not only have I tried to strip away variations which I consider ir- relevant, but I have made specific recommendations against each of White's possible sixth moves. The danger with this approach is that subsequently it might be found that the particular system I am recom- mending is unsound. Possibly, but I doubt it. My recom- mendations are based on my own ex- periences on the tournament circuit. In other words, they have all been road-tested (and very often been in for an overhaul before being wheeled out again). From time to time one is bound to lose a game with the opening — when the position becomes sharp accidents can hap- pen. But don’t throw the whole thing out just because of one bad experi- ence; it is the only way to learn. Have a little faith: go back to the drawing board and find an improvement. There will be one, I guarantee it — the opening is sound! If in the unlikely event a variation is looking dubious, or perhaps if you feel that it doesn’t particularly suit your style, I have given several alter- natives to choose from. But don’t be rail-roaded into playing something you are not keen on, or slavishly fol- low my ideas without thinking. Let me quote from the pioneer of the Russian school of chess Mikhail Chigorin, writing in 1901: ‘Not infrequently . . . the theoretical is a synonym of the stereotyped. For the “theoretical” in chess is nothing 6 Preface more than that which can be found in the textbooks and to which players try to conform because they cannot think up anything better or equal, anything original’, and later on: ‘In general, the game of chess is much richer than is to be gathered from the existing theory, which en- deavours to compress it within defi- nite narrow bounds. ’ To avoid falling into this trap, I have tried to point out the salient fea- tures of the position so that it is pos- sible to develop one’s own ideas based on a good general understanding. Having taken far too long writing this book already, I have just one re- gret: that I didn’t spend longer. At the time of writing, Bobby Fischer has just come out of retirement and beaten Boris Spassky — but sadly Spassky did not have the courage to play an open Sicilian against him. It would be fascinating to see how Fis- cher handles the Najdorf after a break of twenty years, but alas, I'm already months over the deadline, so his new ideas will have to wait for the second edition. Till then, good luck. Najdorf was born in Poland as Mieczyslaw (Moishe) Najdorf in 1910, but remained in Argentina at the end of the Buenos Aires Olym- piad in 1939 — along with the rest of the Polish team and many others be- sides — as war had broken out in l:urope. With a new name and a new life, he emerged as one of the strong- cst players in the Western world in the 1950s. Playing for Argentina in the Olympiads at Dubrovnik 1950 and Helsinki 1952, he made the best overall score on board one. The first recorded game we have of Najdorf using ‘his’ variation is against Antonio Rico in the radio- telegraph match Spain-Argentina, held on 8 December 1949. As is so often the case, it is not the originator of an idea who gets the credit, but the one who sees its merit and is able to apply it success- fully — Najdorf was strong enough to do that. In fact, several Czecho- slovakian players had analysed the idea of playing an early . . . e5 in the 1940s, in particular, International 10 Introduction Master Karel Opocensky (1892- 1975). Hal+ and. . . 2. So where exactly did White make his mistake? Dare I say it, on move three! 1 ed c52 DM d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 &xd4 (6) AD 12 Introduction ‘Almost everybody plays 3 d4. But isn’t this a positional error? I am not joking. I like my centre pawns, and I like a queen’s pawn better than a queen’s bishop pawn! I know that sometimes White sacrifices a knight on dS or e6 and smashes Black before he can castle, but in those games where this has been done, haven’t improvements always been found for Black afterwards? Well then, isn’t 3 d4 something like a cheap trap? I know it can be com- bined with purely strategical ideas, but I find it easier to discuss strategy when I have an extra centre pawn. ’ (Bent Larsen in How to Open a Chess Game; RHM, 1974). White has exchanged off his d- pawn for the black c-pawn, position- ally a risky decision, as Black now has a majority of pawns in the centre. The previous game is an excellent illustration of the advantages this confers on Black: having broken out with 15. . . d5! his e-pawn gave him control of the centre; this enabled him to seize the initiative, which he used to force a transposition into an ending where the extra centre pawn proved decisive. This is a common scenario in the Najdorf variation — much more common than those blinding sacrificial attacks by White which editors like to put in their magazines. If White had had the chance to play his pawn to c4, then he could have maintained greater control over the centre. That is why Black plays 4. 56! straight away, forcing 5 &c3 blocking the pawn. (5 £3 and 5 £43 allow Black to liberate his posi- tion immediately with 5. . e5 and 6. . . ds. ) Unfortunately, it isn’t always so easy for Black to prove his strategic advantage — as Najdorf himself be- gan to discover. While picking up some notable scalps he also had some set-backs, such as the next game. In many variations of the Sicilian (the Scheveningen for instance — 1 e4 cS 2 &)f3 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 HHxd4 56 5 &\c3 e6) White manages to play e4- e5! in the middlegame, when the pawn acts as a spearhead for an at- tack against Black’s king. Of course once Black plays . . . e5, this is ruled out. Against that, it should be said that . . . 5 leaves a backward d-pawn. It is actually very rare that this pawn turns out to be weak; the real draw- back is the weak square on dS — as we saw so graphically in the last game. But don’t worry, Najdorf’s fate can be avoided! The strongest players in the world soon recognised the viability of the system. Tigran Petrosian, the doyen of positional chess in the 1950s and 1960s, made the Najdorf a central part of his opening repertoire. 14 Introduction Averbakh-Petrosian USSR Ch. , Tbilisi 1959 1 e4c5 2 &f3 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 Hxd4 96 5 %)c3 a6 6 Ke2e5 7 Db3 Re? 80-00-09 £5 Keb 10 Rxf6 Kxf6 11 &dS (10) Xl W Ke 5//A7 7 AKA al WAH 7 EE White has played directly for the occupation of dS. Compare the two pawn structures: Black has one pawn ‘is- land’, compact and secure, while White has three. This is no accident, but rather the result of the logical ex- ploitation of White’s strategic error at move three. It is impossible for White to hang on to the isolated pawns and hold the rest of his position together at the same time: 33 Hc3 Wb6 34 Bb3 Wa7 35 Bb4 &g7 36 ha Lh6 37 b3 Ba2 38 Wel Was 39 Wb1 Hal 40 Hbs Wc3 0-1 (White loses his queen in- stead of his pawns) We can see from this game that simple occupation of dS does not puarantee White the advantage — on the contrary, that is often where his problems start. Like someone who overspends, falls into debt, then is desperate to keep up appearances, maintaining the knight on the out- post can be a costly and ruinous business — there is so much more go- ing on in the position. You might well be asking: ‘If . . . e5 1s such a strong move, why not play it on move five? ’ 1 e4 c5 2 &f3 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 G\xd4 D6 5 Dc3 es (13) IAs Wes X "EAR AKA 7 nx a. “mm us LE BAN i A nu AAT BLE 2 Bwge X 6 Kb5+! The only way to exploit the omis- sion of . . . a6. If 6. . . 20bd7, then 7 5! causes disruption in the Black camp, and 6. . . 8d7 is undesirable as the ex- change of bishops increases White's control over the d5-square (remem- ber Geller-Najdorf). Now we can see the point to 5. . . a6 — it threatens . . . e5. Besides that, it ~ - Introduction 15 also prepares the later advance of the b-pawn to attack the knight on c3, undermining the support for White's e-pawn and opening the c-file. Moreover, 5. . . a6 is a fine waiting move. — ‘A waiting move? Surely Black should be trying to develop his pieces as quickly as possible in the opening? ’ True, but here Black can get away with the seemingly decadent 5. . . a6, because he is in no danger of being overrun by White's centre pawns. It is actually very helpful to be able to see White's next move before com- mitting oneself to a particular pawn structure. For instance, if 6 &e2, then there is no reason why 6. . . e5 should not be played; but if White plays 6 Kc4, then 6. . e6! (14) is appropriate, blocking the bishop's diagonal. Hn ERL WES wi A | AKA AL EAM » 3) moo | am man ws This reminds me of a passage in Mihai Suba’s excellent book Dy- namic Chess Strategy (Maxwell 16 Introduction Macmillan, 1991): ‘Alot of ink has been spilt in order to describe White's first move ad- vantage, the tempo up, the theoreti- cal opening advantage, etc. The time of justice has come for Black, be- cause he has his trumps as well, if only the one in the childish joke: “Say a number. ” “Sixteen. ” “OK, seventeen, I win! ” In terms of the mathematical games theory, chess is a game of complete information and Black's information is always greater — by one move! ’ If you would like to learn more about the philosophy of the Sicilian Defence (counter-attack! ) and its re- lated structures, then I would thor- oughly recommend Suba’s book. There is one further aspect of the Najdorf that I would like to mention here, and again, it is connected with Black’s excellent pawn structure. Although I had grown up on Bobby Fischer’s My Sixty Memora- ble Games (Faber, 1969) and seen the many Najdorfs that he played, the opening remained a mystery to me: it just seemed a muddle of variations. It wasn’t until I saw the following game that I realised that Black has an enormous practical ad- vantage over his opponent, namely: Black's king is often much safer than White's. I could point out much better examples of this throughout the book, but at the time, this game made a striking impression upon me. Mestel-Tarjan Buenos Aires Olympiad 1978 1 e4 52 &f3 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 Dxd4 6 5 &)c3 a6 6 Ke2 eS 7 Hb3 KeT 8 a4 0-0 9 0-0 2c6 10 f4 Hb4! There is, however, a great drawback to the move. If we just examine the two king positions, you will see that White’s now only has two pawns for cover, whereas Black's still has three. CERANOE & wi AT 7 AKA X, 2K “swe or The first game I have selected is from 1962. ‘19627, I hear you cry, ‘I want some Hot Theory! ” The prob- lem with just looking at Hot Theory is that it is, to a certain extent, just fashion. Who is to know what the lat- est trend will be in a particular open- ing? For that reason it is vital to see how the ideas in a variation have evolved: without looking back at what has gone before, it is impossi- ble to reason why contemporary players choose to play in the way that they do. And what could be better than looking at a game played by the greatest Najdorf exponent of them all. 20 6Re2 [ Game 1 ] Unzicker-Fischer Va iad 1962 ma Qmpd cp (1 ed ¢52 513 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 Dxd4 £6 5 &c3 ab 6 Ke2 eS 7 Hb3) 7 Reb A good square for the bishop, but 7. . . Ke7 is probably a more accurate move. 8 0-0 Unzicker doesn’t take the oppor- tunity to lash out with 8 f4! , a move which only became popular a few years later. (This, incidentally, is the reason why most players now prefer 7. . Re€7. ) After 8 f4 White threatens f5. If Black now captures on f4, he is then a whole tempo down on Dolmatov’s line (see game 9), so the best move must be 8. . Wc7 — to answer 9 5 with 9. . R%c4. Then it has been shown that 9 g4 gives White a very dangerous attack indeed, e. g. 9. . . exf4 10 25 Dfd7 11 Kxfd {c6 12 Wd2 &ceS 13 0-0-0 Ke7 14 5)d4 g6 15 h4 0-0-0 (kingside castling was hardly possible with the h-pawn screaming down the board) 16 H\dS RKxd5 17 exdS £b8 18 Zh3! (an ex- cellent move: White uses his space advantage to swing the rook over to attack Black's king; Rashkovsky- Gutman, USSR 1974). 8 . Hbd7! Usually the best square for this knight. From d7 the knight protects its comrade on £6, and leaves the c- file clear for the heavy pieces. 9 4 A very natural push, but Fische comments that this is the move he was trying to provoke when playing 7. . . Re6. He believes that Whites e- pawn has now been weakened, as il can no longer be protected by the f- pawn. In recent years, under Kar- pov’s influence, systems where White omits f4 have become more popular. Black must deal with the threat of f5 driving the bishop back. 9 . . We7 10 f5 Ked 11 a4 RKe7 12 Kel 0-0 13 a5! (20) If White had not advanced the a- pawn, Black would have carried out the advance of his b-pawn — if possi- ble all the way to b4. After the white knight moves the c-file would be opened, and the e4-pawn would be weaker. Instead of 13 aS, White had a very sharp move at his disposal: 13 g4. Let’s see what would happen if Black played a ‘natural’ developing move: 13. . 8ac8? 14 g5! £e8 15 Kxc4 Wxcd 16 £d2 (there are other good continuations) 16. . Wc6 17 ds £d8 18 c4 and White has complete control. It is amazing how quickly Black’s position goes downhill if he loses control of dS. Clearly, drastic action is needed. 13. . . h6 doesn’t help much, indeed after White renews the threat of g5 with 14 h4, the situation would be even more serious as a file would be opened for the attack on the kingside. 13. . . dS! is the only correct re- sponse. Provoking the advance of the f- pawn with . . . Re6 is, of course, a double-edged business. With the ex- change of light-squared bishops, Black’s central squares (dS, c4) have been seriously weakened; if White manages to contain Black’s counter- play, he will stand very well indeed. When one sees games like Unzicker- Fischer, it is very easy to be beguiled into believing that the position will turn out well for Black every time. Let us not be fooled: Fischer's play in game 1 was extremely accurate and forceful, one slip and the game could have turned. For this reason I usually prefer not to allow this kind of position: it is not my style to bal- ance on a knife-edge. If White ad- vances the f-pawn, I try to hack it off (. . . exf4) before it can get to f5 — see game 3. The next game might serve as a useful warning. AN WAR ani HABaEA g BEERS Moving the queen out to attack the b2-pawn is very common in the 6Re2 31 Najdorf. It is sometimes seen at a very early stage in the game — the in- famous ‘Poisoned Pawn’ Variation for instance (1 e4 ¢5 2 93 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 Dxd4 6 5 §c3 ab 6 RgS e6 7 f4 Wb6! ? ) — but more often it occurs in the middlegame. The threat to the b-pawn is actually very serious for White. Why? The b-pawn is the linchpin of White's position. Once that goes, then the knight on c3 will have to move; and once the knight moves, then the c2-pawn will be vulnerable to arook on the c-file. An unpleasant chain reaction. [ Game 12 ] Meienhofer-King San Bernardino 1991 (1 e4¢52 53 d6 3 d4 cxdd 4 Dxdd B65 &)c3 ab 6 Ke2 5) 7 Df5! ? (65) Picture the scene. It is the first round of the San Bemadino open tournament. I’m hoping for a quick first game so that I can get outside \ Sa > > SHe MN SS >> N NN > \ AKG Y. . 3 and breathe in the fresh alpine air. My unrated opponent plays 1 e4, and I hit him with the Najdorf. He looks confused. After 6 Le2 e5! he stares at the board as though he has never seen the position before in his life, and a couple of minutes later he plonks the knight on f5 as if it were the most natural move that one could play. Now it was my turn to think. The first thing that occurred to me was that there must be something good against this because no one ever plays it; then having looked for ten minutes and found no clear reply it struck me that if I had seen this posi- tion for the first time, then 7 &f5 would seem the most logical move to play: on b3 or 3 the knight will play no significant part in the game for a long time, whereas on f5 it is already in the thick of the struggle. If Black kicks it with 7. . . g6 then White has the choice of retreating to e3 increas- ing control over dS; or playing the nmbitious 8 £)h6! ? , preventing Black castling for some time. (The Soviet master Rashid Nezhmetdinov once heat Tal using this idea in a similar position. ) Researching what ‘theory’ had to say after the game, the only mention of 7 &fS was in the Encyclopaedia of Chess Openings volume B (Sa- hovski Informator, 1984). But it is worth knowing about in case someone Springs it on you, 6 Re2 57 LL TE WES and it certainly should not have been dismissed by established theory. This just shows that there are many neglected possibilities early on in the game — neglected because players do not have the courage to try new moves out, or cannot be bothered to use their imagination when they could be following ‘book’ moves. Perhaps you are wondering why so much space in this book has been devoted to the 6 Re2 lines. No ex- cuses are offered for this — an under- standing of the 6 Re2 eS lines is crucial to an understanding of the Najdorf as a whole. If you master the ideas in this chapter, then much of the material which appears later in the book will be straightforward. 2 62c4 1 ed c5 2 58 dé 3 d4 cxd4 4 DHxdd fe 5 De3 a6 6 f£cd (69) SARLUEE X 5 A] KARA 7 Al Xm © 7. 0 BAY Uh 7 2 When this move appears on the board in front of me, ‘Danger! ’ starts flashing through my head. Unlike 6 Re2 where White develops quietly and soundly, deferring the main struggle until the middlegame, 6 RKc4 signals an aggressive stance from the word ‘Go’ The bishop aims at the traditionally weak point 7, as well as d5 and e6. The follow- ing joke variation shows that a little care should be taken already: 6. . . 20c6? 7 Dxcb bxcb 8 e5 dxe5? 9 Lxf7+ winning the queen. There- fore. . . 6 e6 . . . blocking the bishop is sensible. (It would certainly not be good to play 6. . . e5 here: 7 @f5! lands Black with a miserable position — White's bishop is perfectly placed on the di- agonal covering dS). White must now use every means at his disposal to get the bishop back into the game, either by pushing his f-pawn to pressurise €6, or, more brutally, by sacrificing a piece. If White fails he will stand worse, the bishop sidelined and irrelevant. This is why the system is so sharp: it is all or nothing. Bobby Fischer took this system from obscurity, honed it into one of the most feared weapons against the Najdorf, and then found a good antidote for Black himself, so that thereafter it virtually disap- peared from top tournament play. Recently, new ideas have been found for White and once again 6 £c4 is in fashion. Fischer’s early games with the variation form the prototypes for White's strategy. After some trial and error, an accurate move order was established enabling Black to develop without catastro- phe. Not only that, a reliable method of counterplay was found. [ Game 17 ] Ermenkov-Portisch Skara 1980 (1 e4c52 M3 d6 3 dd cxd4 4 Dxd4 &f6 5 &c3 a6 6 Rca eb) 7 a3 A move which was in vogue in the late 1950s and early 1960s; but is seldom played now. The reasoning behind this apparent loss of tempo runs thus: ‘Sooner or later a3 will have to be played to prevent Black’s pawn getting to b4, so why not play it immediately? ’ This also allows the bishop to retreat to a2, a slightly safer square than b3. 7 a3 is, however, not only an ap- parent loss of tempo — it is an actual loss of tempo, enabling Black to set up excellent counterplay without any difficulties. 7 Re? 8 La2 0-0 9 f4 bs 10 f5 es 11 Dde2 White will now try to execute Fis- cher’s strategy in Game 15 — the re- moval of everything that covers dS, and then the occupation of that square with a piece. However, Black’s play down the c-file and against the weak e-pawn are more than sufficient to counter White's rather blunt idea. 1 . . £b7 12 Hgd Hbd7 13 fg Ec8 14 0-0 (79) | - White’s threat is clear: Kxf6, & hS, exchange on f6 and then either shop or knight to dS. The situation 1s critical. Black can no longer rely on vague generalisations, some pre- vise calculation is necessary in order 10 avoid falling in with White's wheme. Yet the knight manoeuvre . . . 2d7- ¢5 has a great deal to recommend it. On c5 the knight not only pressurises the e-pawn, it supports the critical e6-point, and if necessary can swap off the bishop on b3. For me, the lat- ter is the most attractive feature of the variation: it is very unlikely that I’m going to fall prey to a violent sac- rificial attack while I have the option of exchanging off the bishop. First of all, let us see what hap- pens if White continues with the plan of Wf3-g3. Kes 6 Rcd 87 I don’t wish to go through any more of this — White has a tremen- dous position and went on to win in forty moves. In December 1991, I had the op- portunity to discuss the position with Mikhalchishin himself. He was con- vinced of the strength of White's at- tack after 9 e5 dxeS 10 fxeS Dfd7, but admitted that there was a flaw in the system. He had shown the idea to his com- patriot Vassily Ivanchuk (who has had some success playing . . . 2bd7). ‘Chucky’ wandered off looking con- fused, but came back half an hour later with what Mikhalchishin claims is an antidote to the whole system. Instead of capturing on eS, the knight retreats to d7 straight away: 9 e5 &fd7! If now 10 We2 dxeS 11 fxeS Whda+ wins the knight. (120) VIEAR ARATE 2 Sw HANA an y 7 g if Now Black may safely recapture 88 6 Rcd on d6. In resolving the tension in this way, the f-file has been kept closed, and the knight returns to f6 instead of creating a jam on d7. A couple of weeks after speaking to Mikhalchishin, I noticed the game Janovsky-Timoshchenko, in which Black used this very same idea. Ti- moshchenko might have found it in- dependently, but it is more likely that he heard it through the grapevine. It is almost impossible to keep ideas se- cret in the chess world for very long. (129) 129 7 7 7 7 VEE x Afi ee 2 . mo . ) | we | Preventing White’s king from coming over to stop the pawns. 35 Hd3 2c? 36 Del ed 37 Ee3 f5 0-1 The 7. . . %)bd7 variation is a fully viable alternative to the older . . . b5. I have found it a very useful weapon indeed, mainly because my oppo- nents have felt unsettled when they 94 6 Rcd face it: there is not a standard recipe that they can trot out because the variation is so new, and theory is still evolving. 6 Kc4 is one of the sharpest ways of meeting the Najdorf, and which- ever variation you choose to play, 7. . b5 or 7. . 2bd7, care must be taken at all times to ensure that you don’t fall for one of White's sacri- fices. However, if you do get to about move fifteen without your po- sition caving in, then the rest of the game should be a doddle. J o6f4 1 e4 cs 2 28 dé 3 d4 cxd4 4 Hxdd De 5 Hc3 a6 6 f4 (130) 110 XJ 2 We % ‘Ea Ehren mr Boman 7 75 7 0 ATSAT TAK BH OPWgL EB There is no mistaking White's in- tentions with this move: he is going to throw it all at us and hope that when the dust settles, it will be checkmate. This is a very dangerous system — but dangerous for both sides. Can it really be good for White 10 throw up two pawns in front of his king at such an early stage in the game? Even when White castles, his monarch has only the h- and g- pawns as cover. The system that I’m recommend- ing against 6 f4 does not prevent White's attack — on the contrary, I would like to encourage it. I am con- vinced that Black’s position is strong enough to withstand the assault, so when White's early aggression has burnt itself out, we move in and mop up the wreck of his position. Itis true that this strategy entails some risk — Black has gone down to some brutal attacks in this line; but just as often, White is left regretting that his set-up is designed only for attacking. First, let us take a brief look at some games in which White's strat- egy triumphs — then we will know what we are trying to avoid. The rest of the game was a massa- cre: 19 Wxh7+ eb 20 Wxg6 We? 21 Hadl Wh722 Wg3 &e723 HHdS5+ KxdS 24 exd5 Whé 25 d6+ a8 26 Kf5 Ha727 2xd7 Exd7 28 Exf6 1-0. Seen enough? In all these games White's attack flowed so smoothly, it was as though Black was facing an inevitable and irresistible tidal wave against his king. It is therefore hardly surprising that 6 f4 has attracted many follow- ers — and having the system adver- tised by Nunn in Beating the Sicilian also helped. Looking at these games, I think Black makes two mistakes: he com- mits his king too early, and/or he commits his e-pawn too early. Don’t get me wrong, it is usually a good idea to castle, and very often . . . e§ can be a good move - often a vital move in attacking White's centre, but the timing is crucial. It is ironic that one has to go back almost twenty years to find a game in which Black constructed a sound de- fensive formation. It is as though this system has been forgotten, lost in the mists of time — or rather, the fog of contemporary theory. So far we have only examined set- ups in which White places his bishop on d3 — and I can guarantee that the vast majority of your opponents will do that if they play 6 f4. There have been so many devastating White vic- tories in this line that it is hardly sur- prising that fashion has swung that way. (I should mention that Black may use exactly the same set-up that I'm recommending against 6 f4 to meet 1 e4 ¢5 2 Df d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 Dxd4 Df6 5 Dc3 a6 6 Kd3 — a rarely played move, but not bad. The majority of games will transpose to the 6 f4 system. ) Actually, it used to be just as common to play the bishop to e2 after playing 6 f4. Though not as overtly aggressive as £d3, some very sharp positions do nevertheless arise. B® wsdl oR This move came back into fashion in the mid-1980s, mainly, I think, be- cause of the attraction of the ‘Eng- lish Attack’: 6. . . 6 7 Wd2 Ke7 8 f3 &c690-0-00-0 10 g4. White's strat- egy is straightforward: the h- and g- pawns will charge up the board with the aim of opening a file in front of Black’s king. If Black dithers, the consequences can be fatal. Murray Chandler, John Nunn and Nigel Short honed this system into a fearsome weapon, and it soon attracted many devotees. After 6. . . 6, the English Attack is, of course, not the only option avail- able to White. He may play another sharp system: 7 g4! ? , inviting the piece sacrifice 7. . . e5 8 Df5 26 9 Kg2 gxf5 10 exfS, a speciality of the late Hungarian master, Bela Perenyi. If Black doesn’t go in for this, then the game will transpose into a Ke. Fe At tack. Alternatively, White ma | play more conservatively with 7 &(2 and castle kingside, transposing into a classical Scheveningen system. So be warned, if you are not happy with the systems that I am rec- ommending, studying all of the above is the alternative. However, there is absolutely nothing wrong with the principled Najdorf move. . . 6 es! Surprisingly, this is slightly frowned upon by older theoretical works. Giving the position a cursory glance, one might think that by throwing the a- and b-pawns down the board Black would have just as much chance of developing a strong attack against White’s king. In this game White wasn’t made to halt his plan for one moment — he might as well have been playing without an oppo- nent. Clearly Black must strive to create some meaningful counterplay on the queenside, in order to distract White from steam-rolling Black's king. It is possible for Black to play . . . b5 in some circumstances, but the timing and move order are critical. A reasonable system is to play as Ribli did in the above game, but to leave the king in the centre for as long as possible. Once again, great precision 6Re3 125 is needed if Black is not to fall prey to a violent attack. The system that I am recommending (hopefully) has the best of all worlds: you get cas- tled; the b-pawn remains on b7, thus avoiding the nonsense with £a5; yet Black still develops strong counter- play on the queenside. Re6. 8 4 has little point when there is no bishop to attack on £5 — Black just castles and follows up with . . . b5! and . . . &b7! , hitting e4 as quickly as pos- sible (compare this with 6 Le2 eS 7 &b3 Re7 80-00-09 f4bS! in chap- ter 1). But in the meantime, have fun. 5 6g3,6h3,6a4 In this chapter we look at three less common ideas for White: 6 g3, 6 h3 and 6 a4. 1 ed cs 2 98 dé 3 d4 cxd4 4 Hxdd ore 5 &Hc3 a6 6 L LE piAE f CHAN NN ARAB Gwping A very solid move. We have al- ready seen how vulnerable White's (over-extended? ) e-pawn may be- come in many variations of the Na- jdorf, so g3, with the idea of £g2 protecting it, is well motivated. The bishop also indirectly controls dS, thus reducing Black’s chances of carrying out the thematic and dy- namic break . . . d6-d5. Of course I'm recommending the Najdorf move 6. . . 5! in this position, but there is nothing wrong with play- ing a strange kind of Dragon with 6. . . 26; or going into a Scheveningen with 6. . . e6. 6 es White has two sensible retreats: 7 &b3 and 7 Dde2. 7 Db3 leads to a slower manoeuvring game, not dis- similar to the 6 &e2 variation — and does not give Black any special problems (see game 45); while 7 &\de2 gives the variation its own character, and is certainly the most challenging of the two moves. As we shall see in the following game, White is able to build a potent attack, though not without entailing some long-term risk to himself. The 6 g3 variation is usually dismissed in theoretical works on the Najdorf: ‘A very peaceful system which is not sufficiently active to cause Black any serious problems’ (Kavalek, 1981); ‘. . promises White no advantage’ (Nunn, 1988). However, because the bishop on g2 provides White with a 138 6g3,6h3 6a4 slightly more stable centre than in other lines of the Najdorf, his king- side attack is launched from a sounder footing. Highly unclear po- sitions may arise. Sergei Kudrin and Alexander Ivanov, among others, have been playing the system for years with Some Success. Popovic-King Palma de Mallorca 1989 683, 6h3 6a4 141 (1e4c52 83 d63 d4 cxd4 4 Hxd4 &Yf6 5 &c3 a6 6 g3 e5) 7 @Db3 (198) 7% mA UOE sEAL rey re LB i “a a v LR ‘ha Bil [RAE @ 2 BWEL EH The Serbian Grandmaster Petar Popovic has specialised in this move for several years. We have played each other three times, and on each occasion we have both felt honour bound to do battle from this position. I have played a different system in each game — for fear that he has cooked up a huge improvement on our last encounter — and each time I have reached a reasonable position. (From now on when we play, to save me the effort of thinking up anything new, I will play these three lines in turn, then every fourth game I will let the Najdorf lie fallow and hit him with a completely different opening — my own version of crop rotation! ) With 7 £b3 White is not attempt- ing an outright refutation of our opening, but rather to creep up on us by stealth and take us off-guard. Don’t worry though, the alternatives are good: a) 6. . . e6! leads to a Scheveningen — but one where White can no longer consider castling queenside, so the dreaded Keres and English Attacks are ruled out. b) 6. . . 26! is also a sound option. Once again, systems where White castles queenside are no longer fea- sible so we have transposed into a classical Dragon. If this Fischer game were more well known than the one against Reshevsky, I wonder how many peo- ple would still be playing 6 h3? 1 ed cs 2 2B dé 3 d4 cxd4 4 DHxdd D6 5 He3 a6 6 RKg5(211) si/Al KAKA BAY a Gwwats C> WW >> =o I suspect that this is the move which deters many people from playing the Najdorf. If one looks in most books on the opening, more space is devoted to 6 £g5 than to any other sixth move possibility; there are reams of baffling variations all looking hideously complicated, and it is simply very difficult to know where to begin. The reason I'm treating 6 Rg5 as just another chapter and not the major chunk of the book is quite simple: in my experience it is not the most popular move. In England (continentals please skip this para- graph), where players are generally less ‘booked-up’ than abroad, I have very rarely had to face the move. White players are scared of the com- plexity and variety of defences (counter-attacks) at Black's dis- posal, and simply do not have the time or inclination to go through books learning masses of variations by rote. Moreover, many of these sharp lines are theoretically good for Black anyway. Bearing all this in mind, I'm amazed that anyone plays 6 Rg5 at all. (The reason they do is for the chance to sacrifice all their pieces in some glorious fashion to force mate. ) Of course, I would not recom- mend playing the Najdorf with a prayer as your only defence to 6 £25. One must have something pre- pared, and there are some very good systems to choose from. Not only that. In spite of the (theoretical) at- tention given to 6 Kg5 in the past twenty years, there are still many 152 6 &g5 avenues that remain unexplored, ne- glected, or wrongly rejected. So there is still plenty of scope for your own research. As we go along, I shall point out the areas where it is possi- ble to stroll from the theoretical path. In the introduction to his book Si- cilian: Najdorf (Batsford, 1976), Michael Stean writes: ‘In contradis- tinction to the majority of lines in the Najdorf which the reader should try to understand, a defence to 6 £g5 simply has to be learnt. ’ This still holds true, particularly with razor-sharp lines like the Polugayevsky or the Poisoned Pawn, but even with the more solid ‘main line’ which I am recommending White usually gets his king to safety on the queenside very quickly, leav- ing him free to attempt either a pawn storm on the kingside; a break- through in the centre with a piece sacrifice; or both. Every move counts, so you have to know what you are doing. But don’t worry, there is logic behind the moves, and it is more a question of learning what one can, and can't, get away with. There are several reasonable ways of meeting 6 Rg5. I think the most principled is the Poisoned Pawn variation: (1 e4 c5 2 &f3 d6 3 d4 cxd4 4 Hxd4 Df6 5 9c3 ab 6 Kg5) 6 . . e6 7 f4 Whe! (212) 212 X F'n oo GAME HATS EWE EE . . . attempting to punish White for moving his queen’s bishop out pre- maturely. If White plays 8 2)b3, then Black has won a moral victory hav- ing driven the knight from the centre. 8 Wd2 Wxb2 is critical, but over the past thirty odd years, Black’s posi- tion has survived repeated attempts to crack it. It is interesting that Miguel Najdorf himself considers that *. . . Wb6 is not in accord with the spirit of the Najdorf Variation how- ever strong the capture of the b-pawn may be. ’ To recommend the Poi- soned Pawn would be equivalent to disobeying Papal authority! The problem with the Poisoned Pawn is that one really must learn variations off pat — a great many — and it is pos- sible to lose a game just because one is not up with the latest theory. Be- sides, it would come into conflict with the title of this book — there are many forced variations ending in White (and sometimes Black) deliv- ering perpetual check. I have rejected Polugayevsky's variation (6 Rg5 67 f4 b5 8 e5 dxe5 9 fxeS Wc7 etc. ) outright. It is just too risky. Not even the man himself is playing it these days. Recently some Soviet players, headed by Kasparov and Yudasin, have revived 6 &g5 e6 7 f4 Wc7! ? . Although Black has done reasonably well with it so far, the critical lines have not been touched upon, so I'm leaving it alone. Once again, it is all very sharp. Kasparov can get away with playing it because his prepara- tion is so thorough. Instead, I am going to examine the old ‘main line’ (6 Rg5e6 7 f4 Ke7 8 Wr3 Wc7 9 0-0-0 Hbd7 etc. ), first popularised by Fischer, and a favour- ite of his disciple Walter Browne, among many others. Now we come to the crunch: (1 e4 ¢5 2&3 d6 3 dd cxdd 4 Dxd4 D6 5 c3 a6 6 Kgs e6 7 f4 Ke 8 Wi Wc7 9 0-0-0 Hbd7) 10 gd (256) 25 pry 3 x 3 ANARAL A Ne ATL _AAl T J 0 i J 3 2 o, “ IA SAT “NON A Ji) , gH LH 6 RgS 181 Fasten your seat-belts; from here on, the ride gets bumpy. White's idea is to take on f6, then push the pawn to g5. As well as cramping Black's minor pieces, this pawn forms the spearhead for an attack on the king- side and centre. Apart from anything else, it makes sound positional sense for White to play like this. We have already seen how effective Black's idea of . . . h6 and . . . g5 is, and this cuts across it completely. But the picture is not all doom and gloom. First, Black's position is very hard to crack: the three centre pawns form a solid protective shell for the king. Second (and on a more positive note), the queenside counter-attack can be deadly. And thirdly, Black usually has the long-term advantage of the two bishops — which admit- tedly isn’t much of a consolation if you get checkmated. So let us see how we can avoid that fate. 10 bs White must always watch out for . . . b4 which can throw his position into disarray. Unfortunately, it is not possible for Black to get in . . . h6 and . . . g5: 10. . h6 11 Rxf6! (11 Khd4? g5! ) 11. . 9xf6 12 h4 and 13 g5. The pawn on h6 only helps White open lines for his attack. 11 Lxf6 Dxf6 11. . 8xf6? 12 §dxbS! axbs 13 &xb5 and 14 Hxd6+ with a lethal attack. The idea of 16. . . 2e5 got a second testing in the German league game Mudelsee-Busch, 1989, and came through with flying colours: 16. . . 2eS 17 WhS (no better than 17 &dS) 17. . %cxd3+ 18 cxd3 Hg6 19 Hes Wes 20 Wid (20 exf7+ &xf7 21 Hxf8+ &xf8 22 Wxg6 Wxdd) 20. . . &xe6 21 D5 0-0-0. Black is a piece up for nothing. Later on, he even won the rook on g8 by means of the manoeuvre . . . Wh1-h7. If you have to face 11 h4, don’t worry if you cannot remember all this — if it is any consolation, by next week I am sure I will have forgotten it all too! However, when sitting at the board it is very reassuring to know that there is a refutation — more than one, probably — and the position is not so difficult that it can’t be re- discovered. Only recom- mended for Fischer freaks. 12 g5 Dd7 13 £5 (257) = - 257 Xx L It is very important for White to plough on as quickly as possible. If he waits for one moment — let’s say with 13 bl — then Black will seize the opportunity to play 13. . . b4! 14 H\ce2 Kb7, followed by . . . 2xS, with great pressure on White's centre. It is possible for White to prevent 13. . . b4 with 13 a3, but this allows Black to start a very dangerous attack with 13. . Bb8! followed by . . . b4. This was popular in the early 1970s for White, but not surprisingly has gon¢ completely out of fashion. After 13 £5 Black faces the diffi- cult decision of what to do about his e-pawn. The most natural move in the po- sition looks to be 13. . . 2e5, but after 14 Wg3, White is able to put too much pressure on e6 (with £h3 and &\c3-e2-f4). Eventually this bastion of Black’s position will fall. If Black plays 13. . . eS, then 14 f6! exd4 15 &dS and 16 fxe7 is very good for White. The most principled move is 13. . %)c5, lending further support to the e6-pawn and leading to great complications after 14 f6 gxf6 15 gxf6 Lf8. This position is currently one of the most hotly de- bated in the Najdorf; White is scor- ing very well at the moment, but I believe Black’s resources to be ade- quate. At first, White players were a lit- tle over-zealous in their attempts to destroy Black's position.
